# Literature and paper copies

This release catalogs **48 documents**: **32 unmodified PDFs included**, with original links for the others. This is a document-access catalog, not the 35-group analytical denominator.

Each included document has an explicit license in its original PDF or official source record. All PDF bytes are unchanged; SHA-256, source URL and license evidence are recorded in [papers_catalog.csv](papers_catalog.csv). Full titles and author bylines remain in the PDFs; historical filenames in this index are labels, not replacement bibliographic citations.

**Third-party rights:** each PDF retains its original license and copyright. CC-BY attribution is retained in the PDF and this index. The IFAC ChebNet article is CC-BY-NC-ND: this is an unmodified copy for academic, noncommercial study. No translation, redlining or modification of those papers is supplied. No repository-wide license overrides paper licenses.

ASCE journal copies and other documents without established redistribution permission are linked, not republished. An openly licensed conference report is not silently represented as the later journal article. Publisher results cited from a journal must still be checked in that journal version. Institutional access does not, by itself, authorize a public GitHub copy.

Ahmed et al. (2025) is a **post-feedback addition**, outside the frozen historical inventory. Its official Sheffield metadata and file checksum are supplied separately.

# Historical search, screening and grouping

## What is reproducible

The committed CSVs reproduce **Figure 4 of report v0.30**: 47 source records assigned to
35 related-work groups, with categories 27 leak/localization, 3 current-state estimation,
2 forecasting, 2 anomaly/fault detection and 1 sensor placement. Six groups were selected
for detailed discussion. Papers, versions and author repositories describing one method
are grouped rather than counted independently.

`inventory_sources.csv` retains every source ID, original URL, evidence locator and saved
checksum/commit from the historical inventory. `group_assignments.csv` makes all group
IDs, categories and selected/not-selected decisions visible. `repro/screening.py` joins the
files and recomputes counts. The code does not contain the category totals as its counting rule.

## Historical collection strategy

The earlier local search record was an exploratory, AI-assisted literature collection,
not a systematic review or a Scopus/Web of Science bibliometric study. It started from
the BattLeDIM committee, official dataset, benchmark paper and competition reports;
followed citations and author code; then searched for pressure prediction/reconstruction,
graphs, autoencoders, dictionaries, ensembles and related evaluations.

Representative queries retained from that earlier search record include:

- `BattLeDIM L-Town leak detection localization github benchmark`
- `L-Town pressure prediction forecasting github BattLeDIM`
- `"BattLeDIM" "pressure forecasting"`
- `"L-Town" "pressure" "forecasting" learning`
- `"BattLeDIM" "15-minute" forecasting`
- `"BattLeDIM" "github" "autoencoder"`
- `"SAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning" pdf`

The counted set retains original or explicitly modified L-Town studies. Other networks,
general reviews and tools are not in the historical denominator. A method's primary goal
determines its category even if pressure forecasting is an intermediate diagnostic step.
This is a scholarly classification, not a result established by counting code.

## Limits and post-feedback addition

Search engines and publisher availability change; a complete historical result log was
not retained. The collection and classifications are not independently human-validated
merely because the CSV joins pass. The inventory does not establish worldwide priority.

**Ahmed et al. (2025), DOI 10.15131/shef.data.29921204.v1, was missing from the historical
inventory.** Its PDF/link is listed as a post-feedback addition in the literature index; it
is not silently inserted into the 35-group historical count. A future literature revision
must reassess grouping and selection in light of that paper.

| Did you personally inspect nominal/generator files, or were those checks assistant-produced? The new audit establishes file facts without assigning you those actions. | |
| Do you agree that elevations agree while model distances and tank diameters differ? Can you distinguish the model vertical reference from a verified sea-level datum? | |
| How would you explain the unknown outflow in tank balance and level-based pump control? Why is AMR_n1 not total tank outflow? | |

1.3 Own exploratory analysis
Our exploratory data analysis (EDA) uses January-September 2018 only: 273 days and 78,624 timestamps. n1 ranges from 25.67
to 29.36 m. The generator opens PUMP_1 below T1=2.4 m and closes it above 3.9 m [1]. Figure 2 shows how pressure, tank level
and pump operation vary through the day; the mean curve does not describe every individual day.
Figure 2. Own EDA: T1/PUMP_1 at left; n1/n4/n31 at right. Thin lines: individual days; red: clock-time mean.
For 78,621 valid +15 pairs, excluding the final three origins, persistence predicts the current n1 value. Its mean absolute error
(MAE) is 0.0518 m (about 5.18 cm); root mean squared error (RMSE) is 0.0685 m and gives more weight to large errors. These
are our 2018 descriptive results, not final test scores. Correlation with future n1 is 0.9923 for current n1, 0.9230 for T1, -0.0367
for PUMP_1 and -0.1574 for AMR_n1. Pressure changes slowly over 15 minutes, making persistence a strong baseline; high
correlation alone does not show that an extra signal improves a forecast.
Figure 3. Own EDA: six origin inputs and the future label. The label is separated for analysis and never enters X.
2. Contributions of Previous Authors
We searched BattLeDIM/L-Town predictive models and followed citations/code. Related papers/repositories form 35 groups of
related works on L-Town or explicit modifications; external networks, reviews and tools were excluded. The five categories mean
leak detection/location, current-pressure estimation, future-pressure/head forecasting, anomaly/sensor-fault detection, and sensor
placement. This inventory is not exhaustive.

PAGE 3 Mini-Project Milestone 2
Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting
DS675
Andres Arango Martinez
3/5
Figure 4. Own screening: percentages use 35 groups; gold identifies six selected subsets. Primary diagnostic purpose can include an intermediate
future-pressure prediction.
ChebNet, SAM-kNN and AGN are core future-state studies; LILA/GATRes are current-state comparators; EILF is supporting
hydraulic engineering. SAM-kNN/AGN/GATRes generate new L-Town data rather than using the released SCADA. Training was
not reproduced.
Study / task / code
Data preparation and how the method works
Reported results and what they show
ChebNet [3] +5-min
pressure and leakage
diagnosis. Code: [4].
Data: Leak-free nominal simulations, network links and
three past pressure readings; 80/20 train/validation split.
Models: Two four-layer graph networks: one forecasts
healthy pressure (+5 min), the other reconstructs current
pressure from available sensors. Their discrepancy over
seven days helps locate leaks. Graph/scaling options were
compared. Code fits scaling before the split.
2019 diagnosis, Table 1: 19 accepted matches,
138 false alarms, four missed leaks; recall 82.61%,
EUR326,521. A match requires a location within 300
m while the leak is active. Many false alarms remain;
these results do not measure forecast-pressure error.
SAM-kNN [5] +5-min
virtual pressure;
anomalies. Code: [6];
scenarios not shared.
Data: Ten new Area A simulations (3 months each; 29
sensors). Code averages three past readings from other
sensors to predict the target’s next reading (+5 min);
paper describes averaging four. Model: Adaptive
nearest-neighbor regression stores recent/older examples
and selects the memory with the lowest tracked prediction
error.
Five leak cases, Table 1: Each leak is detected,
with 3-48 false alarms per scenario versus
11,146-19,216 for ordinary online kNN. These are
alarm counts, not pressure errors. The scenarios are
newly simulated, not the released 2018-2019 series.
AGN [7] Multistep
future nodal heads.
Code: no repository
link found in the
inspected paper version;
data on request.
Data: One generated year on modified L-Town: 5-min
samples, 104 demand patterns; 70/20/10
train/validation/test split. Elevation and water-use
analysis guides features. Model: Graph links share
information; recurrent units retain history; attention
weights nodes/features to forecast head (pressure head +
elevation). Training progressively hides fewer inputs.
Future heads, Table 1: 12/24/48/96-step outputs
reach +1/+2/+4/+8 h. For the 12-step window,
regional MAE is 0.859 m (high), 0.327 m (medium),
32.7 m (low elevation). Errors average whole
windows/regions; +15 is included, but no separate
n1(+15) score is reported.
LILA [8] Current
healthy-pressure
reference; leaks. Code:
[9].
Data: Released SCADA; pressure/flow analysis guides
reference-sensor selection. Leak-free calibration periods
and some per-leak node combinations are manually
selected. Model: Linear regression estimates current
healthy pressure from peer pressures/flow. CUSUM adds
persistent deviations to detect leaks, then hydraulic
calculations locate them. Paper: squared flow; code: raw
PUMP_1. It is not a future forecast.
Journal Table 1: 19 new 2019 leaks; four inherited
leaks excluded. TP=17, FP=2, FN=0; precision
89.5%, F1=0.944, EUR307,852. Two locations
outside 300 m count as false positives, not missed
events; recall=1 therefore does not mean perfect
localization.
GATRes [10] Current
sparse-to-full pressure.
Code: [11].
Data: 10,000 simulated L-Town snapshots (60/20/20
train/validation/test split); compare pressure
distributions with time-series test data. Model: Hide
selected readings and fill hidden inputs with zero. Graph
attention weights neighboring information to reconstruct
the hidden pressures at the same time point.
Table 10 (text: 1 week; caption: 24 h): Across
ten training runs, MAE=0.9501±0.0086 m versus
1.5928±0.0050 m for the mGCN graph baseline. The
± is the standard deviation between runs. This
measures current-pressure reconstruction, not
+15-min forecasting.
EILF [12] Engineering
diagnosis, not future
learning. Code: on
request.
Data: 2018 development/2019 test. In Area C, pump
inflow minus the tank-storage change rate estimates sector
outflow; summed AMRs provide a reference. Use 30-min
windows to reduce level-rounding effects. Method:
Separate pressure trends from recurring cycles (STL),
detect sustained changes, then locate leaks hydraulically.
Journal conclusion, p. 11: TPR=47.83%, five
false positives, EUR167,981. These measure leak
diagnosis, not future-pressure accuracy. The
tank-balance calculation is an existing engineering
contribution relevant to our feature selection.
TP: correctly identified leaks; FP: false alarms/incorrect matches; FN: missed leaks. TPR: recall; F1: balance between precision
and recall. EUR: competition monetary score [2].
Research gap. Among the reviewed studies, none reports a directly comparable n1(+15) forecast evaluation on the released
SCADA. Leak diagnosis, current reconstruction and forecasts with other data/horizons answer different questions. We therefore
compare Gradient Boosting, persistence, Ridge and Random Forest on common chronological splits and test auxiliary-signal value.
This provides reproducible classical baselines for future comparison with complex models, which are not reproduced here.

Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting

1.3 Own exploratory analysis
Our exploratory data analysis (EDA) uses January-September 2018 only: 273 days and 78,624 timestamps. n1 ranges from 25.67
to 29.36 m. The generator opens PUMP_1 below T1=2.4 m and closes it above 3.9 m [1]. Figure 2 shows how pressure, tank level
and pump operation vary through the day; the mean curve does not describe every individual day.
Figure 2. Own EDA: T1/PUMP_1 at left; n1/n4/n31 at right. Thin lines: individual days; red: clock-time mean.
For 78,621 valid +15 pairs, excluding the final three origins, persistence predicts the current n1 value. Its mean absolute error
(MAE) is 0.0518 m (about 5.18 cm); root mean squared error (RMSE) is 0.0685 m and gives more weight to large errors. These
are our 2018 descriptive results, not final test scores. Correlation with future n1 is 0.9923 for current n1, 0.9230 for T1, -0.0367
for PUMP_1 and -0.1574 for AMR_n1. Pressure changes slowly over 15 minutes, making persistence a strong baseline; high
correlation alone does not show that an extra signal improves a forecast.
Figure 3. Own EDA: six origin inputs and the future label. The label is separated for analysis and never enters X.
2. Contributions of Previous Authors
We searched BattLeDIM/L-Town predictive models and followed citations/code. Related papers/repositories form 35 groups of
related works on L-Town or explicit modifications; external networks, reviews and tools were excluded. The five categories mean
leak detection/location, current-pressure estimation, future-pressure/head forecasting, anomaly/sensor-fault detection, and sensor
placement. This inventory is not exhaustive.

PAGE 3 Mini-Project Milestone 2
Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting
DS675
Andres Arango Martinez
3/5
Figure 4. Own screening: percentages use 35 groups; gold identifies six selected subsets. Primary diagnostic purpose can include an intermediate
future-pressure prediction.
ChebNet, SAM-kNN and AGN are core future-state studies; LILA/GATRes are current-state comparators; EILF is supporting
hydraulic engineering. SAM-kNN/AGN/GATRes generate new L-Town data rather than using the released SCADA. Training was
not reproduced.
Study / task / code
Data preparation and how the method works
Reported results and what they show
ChebNet [3] +5-min
pressure and leakage
diagnosis. Code: [4].
Data: Leak-free nominal simulations, network links and
three past pressure readings; 80/20 train/validation split.
Models: Two four-layer graph networks: one forecasts
healthy pressure (+5 min), the other reconstructs current
pressure from available sensors. Their discrepancy over
seven days helps locate leaks. Graph/scaling options were
compared. Code fits scaling before the split.
2019 diagnosis, Table 1: 19 accepted matches,
138 false alarms, four missed leaks; recall 82.61%,
EUR326,521. A match requires a location within 300
m while the leak is active. Many false alarms remain;
these results do not measure forecast-pressure error.
SAM-kNN [5] +5-min
virtual pressure;
anomalies. Code: [6];
scenarios not shared.
Data: Ten new Area A simulations (3 months each; 29
sensors). Code averages three past readings from other
sensors to predict the target’s next reading (+5 min);
paper describes averaging four. Model: Adaptive
nearest-neighbor regression stores recent/older examples
and selects the memory with the lowest tracked prediction
error.
Five leak cases, Table 1: Each leak is detected,
with 3-48 false alarms per scenario versus
11,146-19,216 for ordinary online kNN. These are
alarm counts, not pressure errors. The scenarios are
newly simulated, not the released 2018-2019 series.
AGN [7] Multistep
future nodal heads.
Code: no repository
link found in the
inspected paper version;
data on request.
Data: One generated year on modified L-Town: 5-min
samples, 104 demand patterns; 70/20/10
train/validation/test split. Elevation and water-use
analysis guides features. Model: Graph links share
information; recurrent units retain history; attention
weights nodes/features to forecast head (pressure head +
elevation). Training progressively hides fewer inputs.
Future heads, Table 1: 12/24/48/96-step outputs
reach +1/+2/+4/+8 h. For the 12-step window,
regional MAE is 0.859 m (high), 0.327 m (medium),
32.7 m (low elevation). Errors average whole
windows/regions; +15 is included, but no separate
n1(+15) score is reported.
LILA [8] Current
healthy-pressure
reference; leaks. Code:
[9].
Data: Released SCADA; pressure/flow analysis guides
reference-sensor selection. Leak-free calibration periods
and some per-leak node combinations are manually
selected. Model: Linear regression estimates current
healthy pressure from peer pressures/flow. CUSUM adds
persistent deviations to detect leaks, then hydraulic
calculations locate them. Paper: squared flow; code: raw
PUMP_1. It is not a future forecast.
Journal Table 1: 19 new 2019 leaks; four inherited
leaks excluded. TP=17, FP=2, FN=0; precision
89.5%, F1=0.944, EUR307,852. Two locations
outside 300 m count as false positives, not missed
events; recall=1 therefore does not mean perfect
localization.
GATRes [10] Current
sparse-to-full pressure.
Code: [11].
Data: 10,000 simulated L-Town snapshots (60/20/20
train/validation/test split); compare pressure
distributions with time-series test data. Model: Hide
selected readings and fill hidden inputs with zero. Graph
attention weights neighboring information to reconstruct
the hidden pressures at the same time point.
Table 10 (text: 1 week; caption: 24 h): Across
ten training runs, MAE=0.9501±0.0086 m versus
1.5928±0.0050 m for the mGCN graph baseline. The
± is the standard deviation between runs. This
measures current-pressure reconstruction, not
+15-min forecasting.
EILF [12] Engineering
diagnosis, not future
learning. Code: on
request.
Data: 2018 development/2019 test. In Area C, pump
inflow minus the tank-storage change rate estimates sector
outflow; summed AMRs provide a reference. Use 30-min
windows to reduce level-rounding effects. Method:
Separate pressure trends from recurring cycles (STL),
detect sustained changes, then locate leaks hydraulically.
Journal conclusion, p. 11: TPR=47.83%, five
false positives, EUR167,981. These measure leak
diagnosis, not future-pressure accuracy. The
tank-balance calculation is an existing engineering
contribution relevant to our feature selection.
TP: correctly identified leaks; FP: false alarms/incorrect matches; FN: missed leaks. TPR: recall; F1: balance between precision
and recall. EUR: competition monetary score [2].
Research gap. Among the reviewed studies, none reports a directly comparable n1(+15) forecast evaluation on the released
SCADA. Leak diagnosis, current reconstruction and forecasts with other data/horizons answer different questions. We therefore
compare Gradient Boosting, persistence, Ridge and Random Forest on common chronological splits and test auxiliary-signal value.
This provides reproducible classical baselines for future comparison with complex models, which are not reproduced here.

# Entregables del MiniProject

El usuario exige que todos los entregables conserven el mismo formato visual.

- La referencia es `Milestone 1/Milestone1.tex` y su PDF evaluado.
- Consultar `Plantilla/ESTANDAR_VISUAL.md` y usar `Plantilla/milestone_visual.tex` para los informes posteriores.
- Conservar tipografía, márgenes, paleta, encabezados, tablas y recuadros; cambiar solo contenido, metadatos y estado de la etapa.
- Separar contenido y formato. No introducir un diseño alternativo al generar un nuevo milestone o versión.
- Conservar originales y versiones anteriores. Un borrador no se marca como entregado o aprobado sin evidencia.
- Antes de publicar/entregar, compilar y revisar visualmente todas las páginas; comprobar tablas, desbordamientos, referencias y longitud requerida.

 LILA PAGES 16
A Sequential Pressure-Based Algorithm for Data-Driven
Leakage Identification and Model-Based Localization in
Water Distribution Networks
Ivo Daniel1; Jorge Pesantez, A.M.ASCE2; Simon Letzgus3;
Mohammad Ali Khaksar Fasaee, S.M.ASCE4; Faisal Alghamdi, S.M.ASCE5;
Emily Berglund, M.ASCE6; G. Mahinthakumar, M.ASCE7; and Andrea Cominola, A.M.ASCE8
Abstract: Leakages in water distribution networks (WDNs) are estimated to globally cost 39 billion USD=year and cause water and revenue
losses, infrastructure degradation, and other cascading effects. Their impacts can be prevented and mitigated with prompt identification and
accurate leak localization. In this work, we propose the leakage identification and localization algorithm (LILA), a pressure-based algorithm
for data-driven leakage identification and model-based localization in WDNs. First, LILA identifies potential leakages via semisupervised
linear regression of pairwise sensor pressure data and provides the location of their nearest sensors. Second, LILA locates leaky pipes relying
on an initial set of candidate pipes and a simulation-based optimization framework with iterative linear and mixed-integer linear program-
ming. LILA is tested on data from the L-Town network devised for the Battle of Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods. Results show that
LILA can identify all leakages included in the data set and locate them within a maximum distance of 374 m from their real location. Abrupt
leakages are identified immediately or
TERM 19 PAGE 1 374 m from their real location. Abrupt leakages are identified immediately or within 2 h, while more time is required to raise alarms on incipient leakages. DOI: 10.1061/(ASCE) WR.1943-5452.0001535. © 2022 American Society of Civil Engineers. Author keywords: BattLeDIM; Leakage detection; Water distribution networks (WDN); Change point detection; Successive linear approximation. LILA correctly identified and located 17 of the 19 leaks that started in 2019. All leaks were actually correctly identified, but two leaks were located outside of the search radius umax. This good performance is reflected in a Recall value of 1.0 and slightly lower Precision and F1-score due to the inaccurate detection of two leaks. In terms of aggregate score S, LILA obtained a score of €307,852, outperforming the BattLeDIM benchmark, which obtained a total score of €191,055.  GATRes PAGES 27
Graph Neural Networks for Pressure Estimation in Water
Distribution Systems
Huy Truong1
, Andrés Tello1, Alexander Lazovik1, and Victoria Degeler2
1Bernoulli Institute, University of Groningen, Groningen, The Netherlands, 2Informatics Institute, University of
Amsterdam, Amsterdam, The Netherlands
Abstract Pressure and flow estimation in water distribution networks (WDNs) allows water management
companies to optimize their control operations. For many years, mathematical simulation tools have been the
most common approach to reconstructing an estimate of the WDNs hydraulics. However, pure physics‐based
simulations involve several challenges, for example, partially observable data, high uncertainty, and extensive
manual calibration. Thus, data‐driven approaches have gained traction to overcome such limitations. In this
work, we combine physics‐based modeling and graph neural networks (GNN), a data‐driven approach, to
address the pressure estimation problem. Our work has two main contributions. First, a training strategy that
relies on random sensor placement making our GNN‐based estimation model robust to unexpected sensor
location changes. Second, a realistic evaluation protocol that considers real temporal patterns and noise injection
to mimic the uncertainties intrinsic to real‐world scenarios. As a result, a new state‐of‐the‐art model, GAT with
Residual Connections, for pressure estimation is available. Our model surpasses the performance of previous
studies on several WDNs

Nevertheless, these pressure sensors are limited in practice due to infrastructural limits and privacy concerns. Consequently, the gathered data, known as the pressure states of the junction nodes, are scarce. These states play crucial roles as samples in training data‐driven approaches aligned with a machine‐learning model that often requires a large amount of data. TERM 19 PAGE 6 l‐based GNNs in which the learned features extracted from Fourier space are closely associated with the corresponding network, yielding the lack of reusability (S. Zhang et al., 2019). For this reason, it fails to satisfy (C1)Generalizability. Ashraf et al. (2023) improved the above work on historical data. In this case, working with a spatial‐based GNN can help extract features from any encountering topology, so it is possible to satisfy (C1)Generalizability. In addition, testing on noisy time‐relevant data could be seen as an uncertainty consideration. However, this work heavily depends on historical data generated from a pure mathematical simulation engaging with “unch
TERM 19 PAGE 7 ents, and real‐world data is highly volatile. For example, a model trained on data created from past patterns can fail to estimate the pressure of a WDN during the long‐term COVID‐19 pandemic due to the un- expected sudden change in water consumption that was never found in such patterns (Campos et al., 2021; Tiedmann et al., 2022). Furthermore, the number of available patterns is seldom provided or partially accessible due to privacy‐related concerns, especially in public benchmark WDNs. For this reason, they are often repeti- tively overused in modeling large‐scale water networks where the number of nodes is exponential compared to the required patterns. This significan

 Multilayer Perceptron (Zeng et al., 2020). Ideally, ⊕is designed to propagate messages from surrounding nodes in a sparse fashion, which only matters to non‐zero values. Thus, this scheme efficiently scales when dealing with enormous graphs and economically saves the memory allocation budget. Next, we explain GAT in view of a target node i. Concretely, GAT focuses on the intermediate representation relationship between the target node i and one of its 1‐hop neighbors j. If a node pairs w
TERM 19 PAGE 10 dal pairs. Moreover, we consider the distribution of pressure values in the neighborhood, so we empirically apply a mean aggregator to the current representations (K. Xu et al., 2019). Afterward, we use a non‐parametric residual connection with the intermediate input xi that allows depth extension and diminishes the overfitting problem (He et al., 2016). As in Figure 3, the overall structure is a stack of numerous GATRes blocks. As each block considers 1‐hop neighborhoods, stacking multi‐blocks allows message propagation to faraway neighbors in the graph. Before message propagation layers, we employ a shared‐weight linear transformation to project the masked input nodal f
TERM 19 PAGE 11 s). GNN weights are updated using the loss derived by the predicted and ground truth values at masked places.  Wang PAGES 12
Multiple Leakage Detection and Isolation in District
Metering Areas Using a Multistage Approach
Xiaoting Wang1; Junyu Li2; Shuming Liu3; Xipeng Yu4; and Ziqing Ma5
Abstract: Hydraulic accidents or abnormal situations, also known as leakages, cause not only water losses but also service interruptions and
other negative effects. To facilitate the rapid response of water utilities and reduce water losses caused by undiscovered leakages, a timely,
efficient, and accurate detection method is required. This paper describes a novel framework for multiple overlapping leak detection and
isolation in the water distribution system. The proposed method has three stages: an estimation stage to extract leak-induced data from the
huge monitoring data, an identification stage to detect the presence of overlapping leaks from multiscale difference series, and a localization
stage to pinpoint the leak hotspots. The proposed method detects seven leakages in real-life networks and localizes 16 overlapping leakages
with an economic score of €167,981 in L-town 2019. Comparisons show that the framework can accurately detect it in a fast response time
and effectively localize the leaking pipeline with good applicability. DOI: 10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001558. © 2022 American
Society of Civil Engineers.
Author keywords: Data-driven approach; Hydraulic model; Empirical mode decomposition; Time series decomposition; Multiscale
difference operation.

To fill these research gaps, this paper aims to develop a novel estimation, identification, and localization framework (EILF) based on the data-driven method and hydraulic model to detect multi- ple overlapping leaks inside the DMA. The EILF comprises three stages. In the estimation stage, a joint estimation method and the time series decomposition are proposed to highlight the flow and pressure features related to leakage. In the identification stage, a novel multiscale differential detection is proposed to reduce the nonstationary random item of residuals and to detect overlapping leakages. In the localization

An Interpretable Ensemble Learning Framework for Leakage
Localization in Urban Water Distribution Networks
Qingfu Li and Ao Chen *
School of Water Conservancy and Transportation, Zhengzhou University, Zhengzhou 450001, China;
lqflch@zzu.edu.cn
* Correspondence: aochen@gs.zzu.edu.cn
Abstract
Rapid and accurate leakage localization in urban water distribution networks (WDNs) is
vital for reducing non-revenue water and supporting sustainable water resource manage-
ment. This study develops a spatial–hydraulic coupled and interpretable machine learning
framework for zonal leakage localization under operational uncertainties. The WDN is first
partitioned into zones, followed by leakage-region prediction using a Stacking ensemble
integrating Random Forest, XGBoost, and MLP. SHAP analysis is employed to characterize
the contribution of individual monitoring nodes to regional classification. The proposed
Stacking framework achieves an overall localization accuracy of 0.9667 and a Macro-F1
score of 0.9666, with relatively stable diagnostic performance under pressure noise. Global
SHAP analysis identifies several mon

### 2.1. Problem formulation

Before discussing the issues and challenges, this study provide a detailed introduction to the symbolic representation of WDS as a graph. The WDS is represented as an undirected graph 𝒢=(𝒱,ℰ,𝒲). Here, 𝒱 is the set of 𝐼+𝐼* vertices representing nodes that comprise reservoirs, tanks and junctions. ℰ is the set of edges representing the valves, pumps, and pipes. 
Building upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. Fig. 1 illustrates the overall AGN architecture. The subsequent sections detail the design and implementation of this framework:

Image: Fig 1

  1. Download: Download high-res image (318KB)
  2. Download: Download full-size image

Fig. 1. The workflow of the proposed AGN model.

(1) Neighbor propagation based on adjacency matrix: By multiplying candidate hidden pressure states (CHPS) 𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 with the weighted adjacency matrix 𝒲𝑣𝑖, the past CHPS of sensor node 𝑣𝑖 is transferred to its first-order neighborhood nodes:((5))̅𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝒲𝑣𝑖·𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 where 𝑙 is the iteration of recurrent layers, and the initial CHPS 𝑐(0)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 is an initialized learnable parameter.

(2) Long-short-term memory augmented by self-attention mechanism: By calculating the matrix product of the query vector 𝑄·⁡(·) and the key vector 𝐾·⁡(·) of the node sequence (Eqs. (6)-a and 7-a), the attention between node sequences can be reflected. The attention scores are obtained after normalization through SoftMax activation (Eqs. (6)-b and 7-b). The value vectors 𝑉·⁡(·) of all nodes in the sequence are weighted and summed using the attention scores. 𝐷 is the dimension of the concatenation, preventing gradient explosion.

## 3. Experimental setups

### 3.1. Dataset

Real-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.

Image: Fig 2

Problem formulation\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore discussing the issues and challenges, this study provide a detailed introduction to the symbolic representation of WDS as a graph. The WDS is represented as an undirected graph 𝒢=(𝒱,ℰ,𝒲). Here, 𝒱 is the set of 𝐼+𝐼* vertices representing nodes that comprise reservoirs, tanks and junctions. ℰ is the set of edges representing the valves, pumps, and pipes. \\r\\nBuilding upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. Fig. 1 illustrates the overall AGN architecture. The subsequent sections detail the design and implementation of this framework:\\r\\n\\r\\nImage: Fig 1\\r\\n\\r\\n  1. Fig. 5(a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\\n\\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. Intuitively, sensor nodes in central areas (e.g., 458, 549, and 752) enjoy higher weights due to their critical positions, significantly enhancing the overall prediction performance. In contrast, sensors at the network periphery (e.g., nodes 114, 469, and 769) are less important. The concepts of geographical center and periphery do not exist in graph theory; only the concept of connectivity does. In other words, nodes that exhibit better connectivity or play a crucial role in the network’s connectivity were trained with higher weights. Placing sensor nodes at critical connectivity points appears to be key to improving the performance of graph-based data-driven models. The importance related to node connectivity underscores the need to optimize sensor layouts, especially in selecting critical connections. However, further cross-network studies are needed to definitively establish the correlation between sensor placement and connectivity.\\n\\nBy introducing the self-attention mechanism into the gating mechanism, this study broadens the receptive field of sensor nodes and effectively captures global information. Additionally, it deepens our understanding of the working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. These findings provide theoretical support for optimizing existing models and lay a solid foundation for developing new models for future urban infrastructure monitoring and management.\\n\\n\\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (1009KB)\\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\\n\\nFig. 7. Nodal features analysis: (a) Mask nodal type and nodal elevation features; (b) Mask nodal type features; (c) Mask nodal elevation features.\\n\\nAdding any individual nodal feature to the “AGN(-type)(-height)” scenario can significantly enhance the model’s overall prediction accuracy. Specifically, adding only the nodal height feature (Fig. 7(b)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<1.0 m from 0 % to 95.6 %. This indicates that height, a key indicator of spatial location information, provides extensive and fundamental predictive support for the model. Adding nodal type features (Fig. 7(c)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<0.5 m from 0 % to 23.2 %. This significantly enhances the model’s ability to capture specific nodal characteristics and improves prediction accuracy, demonstrating the unique value of type features in refining predictions. Both nodal elevation and type features offer distinct and complementary contributions to model performance. The results highlight the critical role of prior network information in improving model performance.\\n\\nHowever, in water networks lacking detailed nodal information, obtaining accurate nodal height and type data can be challenging. Therefore, promoting and deepening the AGN model in practical applications will require diversified model assistance and data substitution strategies. For instance, nodal height can be approximated using surface elevation, and nodal type can be inferred by integrating urban AOI data with network topology. With the inclusion and integration of more network prior information, the AGN model can better align with water network characteristics, becoming powerful tools for the safety management and distribution optimization of WDSs.\\n\\n## 5. Conclusions\\n\\nThis study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support. By integrating forecasting and interpretability into a single, cohesive framework, it provides accurate and transparent predictions. Compared to other forecasting methods not reliant on hydraulic models, this study demonstrates the superiority of the proposed AGN method in both spatial generalization performance and its long-term accuracy in temporal forecasting performance. The high accuracy achieved further validates the feasibility of predicting water demand states from pressure measurements, offering a novel approach to demand forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents a pioneering effort to address interpretability within complex WDS forecasting models. By interpreting the model, this study gained deeper insights of the internal working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. Additionally, the interpretability framework aids model refinement, improving alignment with actual WDS behavior and enhancing overall predictive and interpretative performance.\\n\\nIn a sustainable and cost-effective WDS, this research offers a valuable tool for efficient demand modeling for numerous cities lacking hydraulic models. Our research findings have practical implications beyond the benchmark we considered: cities seeking to optimize water distribution and enhance the management level of WDSs will benefit from the insights provided by our framework. The findings hold practical significance extending beyond the specific case studies presented: Cities aiming to optimize water distribution and improve management practices will benefit from the insights offered by our framework. Water utilities can leverage the interpretable predictions to enhance confidence in their scheduling decisions.\\n\\nHowever, this study still has certain limitations and gaps in practical applications: (1) potential information leakage during the model tuning process when determining optimal parameters based on model performance; (2) uncertainties related to sensor acquisition errors and infrastructure conditions; and (3) the inability to satisfy all three conservation equations at the same time.\\nThis study primarily provides model-level explanations. However, for complex model architectures, global explanations may be difficult to understand in depth. To fully comprehend the model’s outputs, future research should develop instance-level explanations that align better with human cognitive habits and are easier for non-technical users to understand. Examples of such methods include perturbation-based, gradient/feature-based, and decomposition-based approaches.\\n\\n## CRediT authorship contribution statement\\n\\nZekun Zou: Writing – review & editing, Writing – original draft, Software, Methodology, Investigation, Formal analysis, Conceptualization. Zhihong Long: Supervision, Data curation. Zipeng Zhu: Validation, Supervision. Yu Shao: Writing – review & editing, Writing – original draft, Validation, Data curation. Tingchao Yu: Writing – review & editing, Writing – original draft, Visualization, Validation, Funding acquisition.\\n\\n## Declaration of competing interest\\n\\nThe authors declare that they have no known competing financial interests or personal relationships that could have appeared to influence the work reported in this paper.\\n## Cited by (4)\\n\\n  * ### Spatio-temporal network risk evolution of PM2.5 transmission pathways considering volatility\\n\\n2025, Results in Engineering\\n\\nShow abstract\\n\\nHaze pollution, driven by PM_{2.5}, poses significant risks to public health and environmental sustainability, particularly in urban-industrial regions like the Fenwei Plain, China. This study introduces the Lasso-Cox Dynamic Attention Network (LCDAN), a novel multimodal framework for modeling PM_{2.5} transmission risks. By integrating Generalized Autoregressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity (GARCH (1,1)) for volatility analysis, graph neural networks (GNNs) with attention mechanisms for spatial path representation, extended Long Short-Term Memory (xLSTM) for temporal dynamics, and SHAP for interpretability, LCDAN captures the complex spatio-temporal evolution of PM_{2.5} propagation networks. Using a comprehensive dataset (2019–2023) encompassing air pollutants, meteorological, socio-economic, and geographic data, the model outperforms eight benchmarks, achieving a 5.5% average F1 score improvement and up to 38% for low-risk scenarios. A case study in Xi’an (November–December 2023) demonstrates LCDAN’s ability to identify critical transmission pathways and risk nodes, with SHAP analysis highlighting industrial emissions and economic density as key drivers. The model’s efficiency (42-minute training, <15 ms online inference) and interpretability make it a robust tool for air quality management, offering actionable insights for policymakers to mitigate haze pollution effectively.\\n\\n  * ### Diagnosing the Roles of Meteorological Forcing, Storage Memory, and Network Connectivity in Cold-Region Streamflow Prediction\\n\\n2026, Water Resources Management\\n\\n  * ### Enhanced Coordinated Sensor Network for Real-Time Lake Kivu Water Quality Monitoring\\n\\n2026, International Journal of Safety and Security Engineering\\n\\n  * ### A physics-informed AI methodology for Prognostics and Health Management of complex systems under low data constraints\\n\\n2026, Journal of Intelligent Manufacturing\\n\\n© 2025 The Authors. 5
Experiments
We empirically evaluate our proposed method in an online scenario for detecting
leakages and sensor faults in water distribution networks – all experiments are
implemented in Python1.
5.1
Data
We use a version of the L-Town water distribution network as used in [16], as a
prominent realistic benchmark for anomaly detection – we only use Area A which
consists of 661 nodes, 766 links, and 29 (optimally placed) pressure sensors. We
build and simulate 10 scenarios where the ﬁrst 5 scenarios each contain a single
leakage – we vary position, time and size of the leakage – and the remaining 5
scenarios each contain a single diﬀerent sensor fault (position is varied):
– Scenario 6: Sensor measurement is overﬂowing over time – i.e. it is going to
inﬁnity over time.
– Scenario 7: Gaussian noise is added to the sensor measurement.
– Scenario 8: A constant value is added to the sensor measurement.
– Scenario 9: Sensor measurement is set equal to zero.
– Scenario 10: Sensor measurement is shifted by a small amount.
Each scenario is simulated (using WNTR [8]) for 3 months and pressure sensors
are sampled every 5 min.
1 Implementation is available on GitHub: https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-
kNN-Regressor OnlineLearning WDNs.


 SAM PDF PAGE 9 
 SAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning in Water Distribution Networks
759
Fig. 1. L-Town network [16] – we only use “Area A” where we have 29 pressure sensors.
The data stream of sensor measurements is post processed by using a sliding
window of size 4 – we average all samples dimensional wise in this time window,
so that we end up with 29 dimensional samples.
5.2
Setup
We compare our proposed SAM-kNN regressor (see Sect. 4) to several other
online learning regressors. In order to justify the introduced overhead of the SAM
architecture, we compare its performance to vanilla regressors (kNN regression
and linear regression) wrapped as online learners by using the river toolbox2.
For each pressure sensor, we build a corresponding virtual sensor based on
all other pressure sensors – i.e. we try to predict (using a regressor) the pressure
based on the past pressure values of all other pressure sensors (see Sect. 3).
These virtual sensors are then used for a residual based anomaly detection –
i.e. an alarm (detected anomaly) is raised when the predicted pressure value
deviates too much from the observed pressure measurement. For each scenario,
the processed data stream is fed as batches of 200 samples to the regressors
(realizing the virtual sensors).
For each regressor, we evaluate the performance of the resulting anomaly
detector – since we are interested in the detection of a single anomaly (leakage
and sensor fault), we report true positives (TP), false positives (FP) and false
negatives (FN). Note that the true positives and false negatives are always either
0 or 1 because we only check whether an alarm was raised when the single
anomaly was present or not – however, for the false positives, we count every
single false alarm.
2 https://github.com/online-ml/river.

J. Jakob et al.
5.3
Results
The results for leakage detection, for each regressor and each scenario, are shown
in Table 1. Likewise, the results for sensor fault detection are shown in Table 2.
We observe the same/similar eﬀects for both types of anomalies (leakages and
sensor faults): We observe that linear regression completely fails to detect the
anomaly – this indicates that a linear model is not suﬃcient to model the
hydraulic dynamics in the water distribution network and hence fails to detect
any anomalies. There is only one exception: For scenario 6 the linear model is
able to detect the sensor fault – recall that in this particular scenario the sen-
sor fault is characterized by a slowly overﬂowing sensor measurements (i.e. the
pressure value goes to inﬁnity), which is expected to be easily detected because
it is a very “loud” fault. The kNN model shows good performance in detecting
the anomaly but has a huge number of false positives – i.e. it is too sensitive and
raises lots of false alarms. Our proposed SAM-kNN shows the best performance
– it is able to consistently detect the anomalies while having a small number
of false positives only. The huge reduction of the false positives in comparison

two networks (reconstructor and predictor), and ii) the
conversion and analysis of the residual.
2.1 Topological Graph Construction
We start with modeling a WDN (topological objects of
junctions connected by pipe segments) using a graph, as
shown in Fig. 2.
Given an undirected graph G = (V, E), V is a set of nodes
where each v ∈V denotes a node on the graph (junctions
of the WDN) and we consider N nodes (i.e., |V| = N);
E ⊆V × V is a set of edges with eij ∈E connecting node
i and node j. It is worth noting that water in WDNs
is characterised by a certain direction of flow, calling
for directed graphs; instead in this paper we consider
an undirected graph. Using undirected graphs was shown
to yield satisfactory results for pressure reconstruction
in Hajgató et al. (2021) and will thus be adapted here
as it has more applicability for real-life situations. As
novel contribution in the graph construction, we introduce
self-loops for the node sensors. This increases the node
degree of pressure sensor nodes in the WDN, amplifying
the influence of the sensor nodes during reconstruction.
Experimentally, higher node degrees graph yield to lower
detection errors than graphs without self-loops.
While G captures the topological structure of the network,
the relation of a given junction to its neighbours is de-
scribed by the adjacency matrix, A. The latter reveals
the connectivity between the nodes of a graph, which can
reflect multiple factors, such as the length of the pipe
separating them, its diameter, the pipe coefficient and
the direction of flow. Three primal weight assignments are
investigated, namely, i) unweighted edges, ii) pipe length-
based weights and variants thereof, and iii) hydraulic loss-
based weights for the pipe segments.
Unweighted edges is the most elementary approach, which
assumes binary weight value assignment as Avu
=
1 if evu ∈E, 0 otherwise. For a pipe length weighted adja-
cency matrix, Avu = lvu, if evu ∈E, 0 otherwise, with lvu
being the length of the pipe between node u and node v.
Finally, a hydraulic loss weighted graph assumes the edge
weight as an estimated head, or energy loss for the pipe
segment calculated empirically using the Hazen-Williams
equation, Avu = 4.727 · C1.852
vu
· d4.871
vu
/lvu, if evu ∈E, 0
otherwise( Hajgató et al. (2021)). The different modes of
weight assignment are treated as a hyperparameter when
training the GNNs, with pipe-length ones found to yield
lowest reconstruction- and prediction errors.
The last step is to define the signal on the graph. Each
node holds a T-dimensional feature vector, which repre-
sents the hydraulic state of the node v over time. The sig-
nal observed at time t at the different nodes on the graph
is denoted by X(t)
G
= [x(t)
1 , x(t)
2 , . . . , x(t)
N ], with x(t)
v
=NaN
if the data is not observed at that location at time t.
Hence, reconstructing and predicting the pressure mea-
surements in the WDN is cast as a problem of graph signal
processing. Given a partly observed WDN, its hydraulic
state is estimated at every intersection of the network by
processing the physical time-series signals in conjunction
with topological information about the piping system. This
effectively describes a graph-signal reconstruction problem
and can be addressed with the application of GNNs. In
the following section, we first introduce the field of GNNs,
motivating our architectural choices. We then conclude the
section with the proposed methodology.
2.2 Graph Signal Reconstruction and Prediction
Graph Neural Networks
GNNs are neural network archi-
tectures that can process geometrical data (non-Euclidean)
with the goal of extracting node features combined with
graph topology to solve tasks such as prediction, forecast-
ing and classification (Ortega et al. (2018)). In the litera-
ture, their architectures can be classified into spatial- and
spectral-based GNNs. The former, implement a message-
passing process along the graph at each graph-convolution
operation, which corresponds to the multiplication of a
convolution kernel with the corresponding node feature
vectors, followed by a sum or a mean rule. These method-
ologies are very simple and adapt to different network
topologies, however they suffer from a well known over-
smoothing problem. In the case of deep networks with
many hidden layers, the extracted features assimilate
across the graph. The latter, spectral-based architectures,
build on multiple hidden layers, each one performing
spectral convolutions, defined from a GSP point of view.
GSP provides a notion of frequency and a graph Fourier
transform, allowing for filtering operation in the spectral
(frequency) domain. As a consequence, a graph convolu-
tional layer, can be written by a sum of filtered signals
followed by an activation function, where each filter is
defined in the graph spectral domain (using an eigen-
decomposition of a graph Laplacian). These networks alle-
viate the oversmoothing effect, while suffering from a large
computational burden induced by the forward/inverse
graph Fourier transform. To simplify the computational
complexity, some approaches based on parameterisation
using Chebyshev polynomials Defferrard et al. (2017) have
been proposed, known as the ChebNet model.
Our Architecture
For a very sparsely monitored WDN,
as the one in our work, a deeper network, consisting of
more hidden layers, might be needed. At the same time,
to ensure a localisation of the leakage, we need to preserve
as much as local information as possible. Oversmoothing
would not allow us to preserve high-frequency (hence local)
information, thus we adopt spectral-based architectures.
Our initial ChebNet model is based on the one provided
by Hajgató et al. (2021) for the WDN of Richmond, which

3. SIMULATION RESULTS
We validate our method on the BattLeDIM, Battle of
the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods, challenge
(Vrachimis et al. (2020)). Its dataset comes from the hy-
pothetical L-Town, with a WDN spanning a total pipe
length of 42, 6 km. The network is segmented into three
areas and composed of 782 nodes. The WDN is further
instrumented with, one tank level sensor, three flow sen-
sors, and 33 pressure sensors. The competition objective
is to detect as many of the leaks that occurred in the 2019
dataset as possible. For this, researchers are provided with
a nominal model of the WDN, contained in an EPANET
hydraulic simulation input file, which has ±10% inherent
uncertainties, due to demand and topological ambiguity.
This nominal model is used for the training of the GNN
reconstructor and predictor, as per Sec. 2.2. Competitors
are further given sensor measurements from the WDN’s
control system related to the year of 2018, along with
a documented leakage dataset for 2018, of when a leak
occurred in the network, when it was fixed and its es-
timated size. The considered leakages include background
leaks measuring 1−5% of the network inflow, medium pipe
bursts representing 5 −10% of the average system inflow,
and large pipe bursts of entity > 10%. The average inflow
of the system is ∼180m3/h. Both abrupt and incipient
leakages occur in the dataset. A scoring function is further
supplied in the challenge to evaluate the accuracy of the
methods, converting the scores into Euros saved from
non-revenue water and reparation costs, by the assessed
detection method. The sooner a leak is detected and the
closer it is to the actual source, the higher the economic
reward. True positives are defined as detection alarms
raised during the lifetime of a leakage and within a distance
of 300 m from the leakage location.
Equipped with this competition dataset and model, the
proposed GNN reconstructor has been trained. For what
Fig. 5. Residual signal of node 1 for the year 2018.
concerns the predictor GNN, a window of past partially
observed measurements is needed for the inference of nodal
pressures at the next time step. The size of the window T
has been selected during the training phase, considering
values of T = [1, 2, 3, 6, 12, 24], corresponding to windows
of 5 min up to 2 hr being used as the input to the GNN,
since the data is sampled at 5 min intervals. A value of
T = 3 was then selected.
The model is then tested on unseen data from the his-
torical pressure measurements of 2019. Fig. 4 shows one
inferred pressure scene for the 782 nodes, from a single
observation of 33 measurements. We then analysed node
residual signals rv(t) at each node v, which are computed
at every timestep. An example can be seen for node 1
in Fig. 5. The edge-wise residual signals of pipes in the
neighbourhood of a leak in pipe 31 is shown in Fig. 6.
As evident from Fig. 6, the signal mean values are not
equal to zero. We deduct the mean value computed at
each single pipe only from observations when the pipe
is known to be in a non-faulty state, and its neighbors
too. Edge-wise residual analysis is then performed on the
moving averages of the signals for leak detection. The
rolling means ¯rv(t) are calculated described in the previous
section and the detection depends on the condition in
Eq. 1. There are two parameters that need to be set in the
detection method: α the false alarm rate parameter that
scales the detection threshold, and the size of the window
for calculating the moving average and threshold.
A window size of m = 7 days and a false alarm rate
parameter α = 1.0 are chosen. The leakage candidates
are filtered, so that no more than a single detection
may be returned on a given day in a neighborhood of
6 hops. The reasoning behind this approach is the in-
tuition that information of the leakage propagates the
network from the leakage origin, thus causing false-alarms
in areas non-related to the leakage. The simulation results
obtained with this configuration led to an Economic score
of e326.521, 19 True positives, 138 False positives, and
4 False negatives, as shown in Table 1. It is worth noting
that with respect to the teams in the BattleDIM challenge,
our method only uses pressure measurements, while other
entries also used flow measurements and smart meter mea-
surements. We recognise that the proposed method suffers
from false positives, with 138 false alarms given over the
year. By an initial analysis, we have observed a strong
dependency of the rate from both the topology and the

Furthermore, let the vector MREs be represented by the MRE

Multiple simultaneous leaks will also be reflected in the time

LI-3: Change Point Detection
The last task of leakage identification is the detection of variations
in the MRE time series induced by a leak flow; therefore, this rep-
resents a change point detection problem. Change point detection
problems have been widely studied in the literature, and there is a
multitude of statistical methods available. For a comprehensive
review we refer the reader to Aminikhanghahi and Cook (2017).
As explained in the previous section, we expect the MRE to be
unbiased and normally distributed for the network state without
leaks. With the start of a leak event, the error mean will either
abruptly or gradually shift away from zero, depending on the nature
of the leak. Successful change point detection aims to flag these
instances as early as possible, while at the same time avoiding false
alarms. In LILA, we apply the widely used Cusum algorithm
(Montgomery 2020), known from statistical process monitoring,
to automatically detect changes in the MRE.
The Cusum algorithm is a method to detect changes in the prob-
ability distribution of an univariate signal. It accumulates the dis-
tance Ci of the target variable x from its mean μx, corrected by a
slack value K, which is chosen based on the change of mean that is
to be detected. Often K is selected based on the estimated standard
deviation σx of the target variable multiplied by a parameter δ
[cf. Eq. (16)]. A change point is flagged once Ci exceeds a prede-
fined threshold H ¼ η × σx. In general, the algorithm comes in two
forms for detecting positive and negative changes from the mean.
For our application, we use the formulation to detect changes in the
positive direction because leaks will manifest themselves as posi-
tive values in the MRE (cf. section LI-2: Reconstruction Error
Analysis)
Cþ

To evaluate the general suitability of the Cusum method for the
leak identification problem, we created artificial error trajectories
by adding different levels of white noise to given ground truth leak
trajectories. This allowed us to confirm the algorithm’s ability to
robustly flag the leaks and to select the hyperparameters in a rea-
sonable range (cf. Appendix). Moreover, we can derive some useful
intuitions. Naturally, the change point method performs better for
higher ratios of leak magnitude to signal noise. This reflects the fact
that a smaller model error and better signal quality enable earlier
leak detection. The same holds for the temporal development of the
leak, as abrupt bursts are easier to detect than slow transients.
Leakage Localization
The leakage localization step has three subcomponents, including
water demand calibration, pressure difference analysis to identify
the pipes with the highest influence on the pressure sensors, and the
application of the SLA method developed by Berglund et al. (2017)
to quantify the approximate leakage magnitude and localize the
leak in the WDN. The three steps shown in Fig. 1 work sequentially
to, first, partially calibrate the hydraulic model using demand data.
Second, a pressure difference analysis is conducted to define the
order of the pipes that affect each pressure sensor of the water net-
work and report them as leak candidates. Third, the application of
SLA aims at finding the magnitude of a leak from the candidates
that could be producing the discrepancy between nonleak and leak
pressure values. A detailed explanation of each leak localization
component is presented in what follows.
LL-1: Water Demand Calibration
Water demand calibration aims at matching the simulated and the
observed demand to replicate real conditions with the hydraulic
model (Walski 2017). LILA requires a demand-calibrated model
to replicate the demand and the pressure measured values reported
by the sensors. To calibrate the demand patterns and base demand
in the WDN hydraulic model, we use automatic meter reading
(AMR) data available for the simulation period. The new calibrated

of the SLA approach is significantly affected.

 Wang PDF PAGE 3 
 that DMA flow is more sensitive to leakage events, and pressure
sensors are more widely distributed in the observation field than
inlet flowmeters. To facilitate the identification of leak-induced
data from the time series monitoring data, the anomalies caused by
the leakage need to be highlighted.
DMA inflow has received wide attention in leak detection given
its sensitivity for abnormal leak states. Leakage is the extra con-
sumption apart from normal user consumption of DMA, whose es-
timation is undertaken by calculating the deviation between DMA
inflow and user consumption. Therefore, the estimation of DMA
inflow, or referred to as DMA water consumption under normal
conditions, is the first procedure for leakage detection and isolation.
This section proposes a joint estimation method based on two
DMAs in the same city, one nonleakage DMA-a is used to extract
the seasonal trend of this city, and one unidentified DMA-b is used
to extract the weekly water consumption in a nonleaking state.
Based on seasonal and weekly changes, the normal water consump-
tion of DMA-b can be estimated.
For DMA-a, water consumption is a typical nonstationary time
series. Previous studies have shown that empirical mode decompo-
sition (EMD) is an efficient tool for analyzing nonlinear and non-
stationary data (Huang et al. 1998; Karthikeyan and Nagesh Kumar
2013). In this study, EMD is proposed to extract the seasonal trend
of normal water consumption. The authors assume that the water
consumption data are composed of trend items (changes within the
season), periodic items (changes within the week), and random
items (by stochastic factors), given as
YðtÞ ¼ SðtÞ × TðtÞ × RðtÞ
ð1Þ
where YðtÞ = normal water consumption of DMA-a; SðtÞ = trend
term; TðtÞ = periodic term; and RðtÞ = random term in the data.
It should be noted that Eq. (1) needs to be logarithmically trans-
formed before EMD. The residual term of the raw signal after EMD
represents the seasonal trend of water consumption.
For DMA-b, the nonleakage week pattern can be extracted by
the demand pattern of the hydraulic model or the average of multi-
ple nonleakage weekly demands. Based on seasonal and weekly
changes, the estimated normal water consumption in the DMA-b
can be calculated by the following method, given as
DemandDMA−b ¼ SðtÞ × qðtÞ
ð2Þ
where qðtÞ = total demand of DMA at time t. We can get the weekly
through the flow of DMA-b when there is no leakage, which also
is a water use pattern in a week. qðtÞ is obtained by repeatedly
extending the pattern to one year.
Another indicator that responds to leakage is pressure. Different
from the flowmeters located at the entrance and exit of DMA, there
are generally more correlated pressure sensors in the same DMA
area (Wu and Liu 2017a; Xu et al. 2020). When a leak occurs in the
pipe network, the pressure at the node adjacent to the leak will de-
crease, and the closer the node is to the leak, the greater the pressure
drop. Similarly, when the leak is repaired, the closer the node is
to the leak, the greater the pressure rise. Therefore, the pressure
increase or decrease, that is, the pressure trend at each node, is used
as the highlight indicator of the leakage in this section.
The seasonal-trend decomposition procedure based on Loess
(STL) is proposed to estimate the pressure trend from ordinary
pressure time series (Cleveland et al. 1990). The pressure time
series P is decomposed into three series including daily compo-
nent D, trend component T, and residual component R, given as
PiðtÞ ¼ DiðtÞ þ TiðtÞ þ RiðtÞ
ð3Þ
where TiðtÞ = pressure trend time series at pressure sensor i in
the DMA-b.
Identification of Leakages
Leakage is the extra consumption apart from the normal water
consumption in DMA. When a leak occurs, the SCADA system
will record online monitoring data containing the leak. The flow
and pressure caused by the leakage are highlighted in the previous
“Estimation of Flow and Pressure” section as (1) the flow residual
between normal water consumption and the actual water consump-
tion monitored by SCADA sensors; and (2) the pressure trend at
different nodes. In this section, the authors use the presented two
features to focus on solving two identification problems: (1) the
detection of leaks; and (2) the assessment of leaks.
The purpose of leak detection is to identify the start time, end
time, and volume of leaks and to provide zonal (or DMA-level) leak
tips for the water utility. Previous studies have suggested that leak-
age is more sensitive to flow than to pressure (Mounce et al. 2011;
Ye and Fenner 2011). Considering the timeliness and accuracy of
leak identification, the flow residual series between normal water
consumption and the actual water consumption monitored by
SCADA sensors is used for solving the first leak detection problem.
For flow residual data, the characteristics of the flow residual
sequence should be analyzed first. Although the residuals series is
calculated by the difference between observed and estimated series,
it is still a time series with irregular fluctuations. To reduce the non-
stationary residual series and amplify the leakage signal implicit
in the residual series, a multiscale difference method is proposed
in this study. Difference operation, also known as finite difference,
difference function, or difference operation, is a concept in math-
ematics. It maps the original function fðxÞ to fðx þ aÞ −fðx þ bÞ.
Difference operation, corresponding to differential operation, is an
important concept in calculus, which is commonly used for the
smoothing of nonstationary time series (Flajolet and Sedgewick
1995). In this study, in order to eliminate the change of the mean
value and highlight the mutation of the sequence over time, the
difference value ΔrðxkÞ of the continuous items in the residual
sequence is calculated by Eqs. (4)–(7)
Δr1ðxkÞ ¼ rðxkÞ −rðxk−1Þ
ð4Þ
ΔrhourðxkÞ ¼ rðxkÞ −rðxk−1 hourÞ
ð5Þ
ΔrdayðxkÞ ¼ rðxkÞ −rðxk−1 dayÞ
ð6Þ
ΔrweekðxkÞ ¼ rðxkÞ −ðxk−1 weekÞ
ð7Þ
The relationship between flow residuals and leakage should be
analyzed. In WDSs, the leakages are usually divided into two types:
abrupt leakage and incipient leakage. Corresponding to residual
data, the pattern of residual series contains constant (no leak),
abrupt increase (abrupt leakage), incipient increase (incipient
leakage), and abrupt decrease (repaired leakage, the end time of
leakage). To identify the leakage signal in the residual difference
sequence, statistical process control (SPC) methods are proposed in
this study. The abrupt increase and decrease are easily identified
through difference transformation, and the three-sigma SPC limit
is used to identify the sudden rise and incline of the flow curve
(Jung et al. 2015; Wang et al. 2020). The three-sigma SPC method
is analyzed by Z score, which is a statistical indicator for outlier
detection. Z score indicates how many standard deviations a data
point is from the average of the sample, given as
© ASCE
04022021-3
J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.
 J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022021 
 Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 10/15/26. 
Copyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. 


 Wang PDF PAGE 7 
 one zone, which is called area AB, because there is no separate
sensor measuring the inflow to area B. The flow residuals in
area C and area AB were calculated separately.
For area C, the difference between the total inflow and total
water consumption can reflect the leak information. The total in-
flow is calculated via the water level in the tank and the flow data of
the pump that delivers water to the Tank. It should be noted that the
level data of the tank has not changed before and after some time
point, and the pump at this time also stopped running. This seems to
suggest no water usage in the area, yet it contradicts the data from
the AMRs that show evident water consumption in that period. This
implies inaccuracy in the level data of the tower. To minimize its
adverse impact on the detection of leakage events in area C, the
total inflow and total demand in area C are calculated at intervals
of 30 min.
The total inflow to the area AB is recorded by three flow sen-
sors. The actual water consumption of users in the area AB needs
to be estimated. Considering the relatively complete monitoring of
junction consumption behavior in area C, this can be used as the
nonleakage DMA data to extract the seasonal trend of the L-town.
Specifically, the changing trend of users’ water usage mode in
area C during the year should be similar to that in area AB.
Therefore, the seasonal trend in the data in area C can be trans-
planted to the prediction of water demand in area AB. The weekly
pattern of area AB can be predicted by combining it with the sim-
ulation in one week of the hydraulic model. The main solution here
is to analyze the trend of demand mode in area C, the flow series of
2018 and 2019 are decomposed by EMD. The seasonal change
trend of water consumption obtained by EMD is shown in Fig. 6.
The figure also shows the value of the 1st/3rd/5th intrinsic mode
function (IMF) (Hu et al. 2021). Each IMF represents the compo-
nent of a certain frequency characteristic in the original signal.
The interannual variation of water consumption can be expressed
by residual. From the interannual point of view, the growth of
water consumption is slow, and the water consumption in winter
is less than that in summer. After that, the inflow could be estimated
through the EMD seasonal component and demand pattern of
DMA, and the result of estimation is shown in Fig. 7. Besides,
the time series trend decomposition model to estimate the trend
item of the pressure series. The pressure trend series is decomposed
by additive mode STL. When different leaks occur, the pressures
of multiple nodes will change in different magnitudes, as shown
in Fig. 8.
Performance of Identification
The pressure and flow changes resulting from the leak are described
by the estimated flow residual series and pressure trend series.
For the flow of area AB, it is intuitive to calculate the residuals
between inflow and estimated flow for identifying leakages. For
the flow of area C, data observed from the pump sensor and the
tank level meter are used to calculate the total inflow of area C,
and the sum of AMR demands gives the aggregate user consump-
tions. The difference between total inflow and user consumptions is
used to identify leakages as residuals in area C.
As shown in Fig. 7(b), although the residuals series is calculated
by the difference between observed and estimated series, it is still
a time series with irregular fluctuations. The irregular fluctuation
proves the importance of taking difference and smoothing methods.
It is worth noting that the difference operation is very sensitive to
the high-frequency components of the data, special attention should
be paid to the influence of noise data. Before the difference oper-
ation, fuzzy noise reduction processing is generally required. To
reduce the nonstationary of residual series, moving average is cal-
culated to make the data smooth, the smoothed flow residual in
2019 Area AB is shown in Fig. 9(a).
Fig. 6. Results of flow series decomposition by EMD method: (a) the 2018 seasonal trend of water consumption obtained by EMD; and (b) the 2019
seasonal trend of water consumption obtained by EMD.

 Wang PDF PAGE 11 
 Conclusions
In this work, a novel framework called EILF is proposed for timely,
accurate, and effective detection and isolation of overlapping leak-
ages. Flow and pressure features are highlighted through the esti-
mation stage, multiple abrupt and incipient leakages are detected
through the identification stage, and the leak hotspots are local-
ized through an improved differential search localization stage.
The multiscale differential operation, a simple but efficient math-
ematical method, is employed to effectively extract leak-induced
mutation, reduce uncertainty, and improve localizing accuracy in
the WDSs. Two case studies, a real-life network and an L-town
network, are applied to demonstrate the proposed method. The con-
clusions are drawn as follows:
1. Applying the empirical mode decomposition and time series de-
composition, accurate estimations of flow under the normal con-
dition and pressure-drop trend are used to extract leak-induced
values from the monitoring data. Rather than analyzing original
flow and pressure signals directly, this study highlights the es-
sential leak characteristics that reduce the impact of diurnal var-
iations and provides a better solution for leak detection.
2. Multiscale difference series are calculated to reduce the nonsta-
tionary random item of residuals, the residual difference series
are used for leak identification. Similarly, the weekly difference
idea is employed to design the objective function for the locali-
zation searching problem. This approach improves the ability to
deal with uncertainty.
3. The equality of the variation of the flow residuals and the rank-
ing of pressure nodes are used to match the start time and the
end time of an abrupt leakage. Meanwhile, the sliding window-
based variation method is proposed to detect the start time and
leak size of incipient leakages. The EILF method provided an
effective multiple overlapping leak detection framework.
4. The method detects and localizes 16 leakages in 2019 with a
true positive (TPR) of 47.83%, a false positive of 5, and an eco-
nomic score of €167,981. A comparative result shows that EILF
achieved a good grade in BattleDIM 2020, demonstrating the
effectiveness of the solution for leak detection and localization
in water distribution systems. The application of this method in
real-life networks also provides a good example for rapid leak
detection in data-driven networks. The result shows that the
EILF shows good prospects for application in water distribution
systems.
Data Availability Statement
The case data and the model used in this study can be made avail-
able from BattleDIM 2020, and the codes for the proposed method
in Spyder3.7 can be made available by the corresponding author by
request.
